UIG projects by the Public Works Department (PWD) had been largely
completed, other UIG projects undertaken by the Delhi Tourism and

Chapter 2 : Performance Audit

2.2.1 Introduction

The Jawaharlal Nehru National Urban Renewal Mission (JNNURM) was
launched in December 2005 with the objective of reforms driven and fast
track development of cities across the country. The mission period was seven
years 1.6. up to 2012. It has been extended up to 2013-14 for completion of
ongoing projects sanctioned till March 2012 only. The main objectives of
JNNURM were as follows:

(i) Focused attention to integrated development of infrastructural services.

(ii) Ensure adequate funds to meet the deficiencies in urban infrastructural
services.

(iii) | Scale-up delivery of civic amenities and provision of utilities with
emphasis on universal access to the urban poor.

(iv) Special focus on programme in old city area to reduce congestion; and

(v) Provision of basic services to the urban poor.

JNNURM consists of two sub-missions:

(i) Urban Infrastructure and Governance (UIG): The UIG component
provides for urban infrastructure projects relating to water supply,
sewerage, solid waste management, road network, urban transport and
redevelopment of inner (old) city areas etc. in 65 mission cities.

(ii) Basic Services to the Urban Poor (BSUP): The BSUP component covers
housing and slum development through projects for providing shelter,
basic services and other related civic amenities in 65 mission cities.

There are, in addition, two other components - (a) ‘Urban Infrastructure
Development Scheme for Small & Medium Towns’ (UIDSSMT) and (b)
‘Integrated Housing and Slum Development Programme’ (IHSDP) applicable
to all cities/towns as per 2001 census. Ministry of Urban Development
(MoUD) is the nodal Ministry for UIG and UIDSSMT. Ministry of Housing
& Urban Poverty Alleviation (MOHUPA) is the nodal Ministry for BSUP and
IHSDP.

ganizational set up / framewo

(a) Central level

The JNNURM functions under the overall guidance and supervision of a
National Steering Group (NSG), chaired by the Minister of Urban
Development and co-chaired by the Minister of State for Housing and Urban
Poverty Alleviation. The NSG is to set policies for implementation, monitor
and review progress and suggest corrective measures, where necessary. The
NSG is supported by a Technical Advisory Group (TAG), whose task is to

का


Audit Report No. 2 of the year 2013

appraise proposals, and a Central Sanctioning and Monitoring Committee
(CSMC), responsible for further appraising and sanctioning proposals.

(b) State level

At the state level, the JNNURM is to be coordinated by State Level Steering
Committee (SLSC), headed by the Chief Minister of States. It also reviews
and prioritizes proposals for inclusion in the JNNURM. The SLSC is to be
supported by nodal agencies that invite project proposals, appraises them, and
manages and monitors the INNURM.

2.2.2 i f i of Delhi

The NCT of Delhi is a Union Territory with Legislative Assembly and has the
character of a State as well as of a Metropolitan City. Three Municipal
Corporations of Delhi (MCsD), the New Delhi Municipal Council (NDMC),
and the Delhi Jal Board (DJB) are the parastatal agencies of the NCT of Delhi.
Delhi has been one of the fastest growing cities in the country, clocking over
47 per cent decadal growth from 1991-2001, more than double the national
rate. According to the 2001 census, the population of Delhi was 1.38 crore and
the density of population was on an average 9340 person per sq km which is
the highest among all States and Union Territories.

greement

Development and the Ministry of Urban Employment d Poverty Alleviation,
Government of India and Urban Development Department, Government of
NCT of Delhi and the Municipal Corporation of Delhi, the DJB, the NDMC
was signed on 11 September 2007 for seeking financial assistance from the
Government of India (Gol).

2.2.3 Performance Audit of URM.

The performance audit of JNNURM in respect of NCT of Delhi was
conducted during the period from April 2011 to December 2011, which was
updated during the period from April to June 2012, covering the mission
period from December 2005 to May 2012.

Audit objectives

The objectives of the performance audit are to assess whether:
> the reform agenda sought to be achieved has been accomplished,

> City Development Plan (CDP) comprehensively outlines the vision
and development strategy for sustainable future development of the
city and has been properly documented,

> there was a critical assessment of needs in each of the infrastructure
areas and the projects were planned appropriately,

> projects were executed efficiently and achieved intended objectives,

> financial management controls were adequately exercised,

> adequate provision for operations and maintenance of created assets
had been made, and

> there was a mechanism for adequate and effective monitoring and
evaluation.

2.2.3.2 Audit criteria

The Audit findings were benchmarked against the criteria derived from the
following sources:

> Guidelines, instructions, circulars, orders issued by the Ministry of
Urban Development, Ministry of Housing and Urban Poverty
Alleviation, and the Ministry of Finance , Gol,

> Memorandum of Agreements (MoAs) and Detailed Project Reports
(DPRs) of selected projects,

> City Development Plans (CDPs) of NCT of Delhi and Toolkits issued
by MoUD and MoHUPA for various issues under JNNURM, and

> Guidance for Basic Services for Urban Poor — Minutes of the various
meetings of CSMC.

2.2.3.3 Audit methodology

The audit was conducted through examination of records in the Urban
Development Department (UDD), parastatal agencies - Municipal

49


Audit Report No. 2 of the year 2013

Corporations of Delhi (MCsD), the Delhi Jal Board (DJB), and implementing
agencies — the Public Work Department (PWD), the Delhi Urban Shelter
Improvement Board (DUSIB), the Delhi State Industrial and Infrastructure
Development Corporation Limited (DSIIDC) and the Delhi Tourism and
Transportation Development Corporation Limited (DTTDC).

The audit commenced with an entry conference held on 13 May 201] attended
by Secretary (UD), Director (Works) PWD, and the representatives of DJB,
DSIIDC, DITDC, DTC and DUSIB to discuss the objectives, methodology
and modalities of the performance audit. A consolidated report was issued to
the Government of Delhi in November 2011. An exit conference with the
auditee organizations was held on 23 January 2012 to discuss the audit
observations. Report was updated up to 31 May 2012 by revisiting the above
mentioned offices/units and organizations and updated report was issued to
Government of Delhi in October 2012. However, reply of the Government
was not received as of March 2013.

We place on record our sincere appreciation for the co-operation of the UDD,
PWD, MCD, 98, DUSIB, DSIIDC and DTTDC in facilitating the audit.

cities by enhancing effective urban service delivery and civic infrastructure.
State Governments and Urban Local Bodies (ULBs) were thus, required to
accept an agenda of reforms to be implemented during the mission period.

2.2.4.1

Preparatory framewor

at State and ULB level

The scheme was to address planned urban perspective framework for a period
of 20-25 years indicating policies, programmes and strategies of meeting fund

51


Audit Report No. 2 of the year 2013

requirements. A perspective plan was to be prepared and followed by
preparation of City Development Plan integrating land use with services,
urban transport and environment management. Funds from Central and State
Government flow directly as Grants-in-Aid to the SLNA. The SLNA
disburses funds for identified projects to the ULB/ parastatal agency as a soft
loan/ grant or grant-cum-loan.

2.2.4.2 Defunct SLNA

The SLSC was to be supported by the SLNA. This agency was to invite
project proposals, appraise them, manage and monitor the physical and
financial progress of sanctioned projects. Audit observed that the SLNA in
Delhi was not in a position to discharge its functions as it had no mechanism
to do so. There was shortage of expert manpower in the SLNA and it was
dependent on implementing agencies for all its functions.

The Department stated (October 2011) that SLNA was in process to appoint
sufficient number of people. Reply clearly shows that approval of the projects
by the SLSC was given without due technical examination of DPRs submitted
by the executing agencies at the State Government level.

2.2.4.3 Non-setting up of Community Participation Fund

A Community Participation Fund (CPF) was to be established to engage the
community in the progress of JNNURM to encourage innovation at local
level. A Community Development Network (CDN), which aims at
participation of poor through network of Community Development Security
(CDS), Self Help Group (SHG) and other community level organizations for
poverty reduction and livelihood development, was to be developed.
However, both CPF and CDN were not established.

The Department confirming the facts, assured (October 2011) that CPF would
be formed soon. However, position remained same in May 2012.

2.2.4.4 Non-integrated approach in project formulation

Though JNNURM aims at integrated development of infrastructural services
in the city, yet 24 projects taken up under UIG, related only to road
infrastructure which were initially planned keeping in view the requirement of
CWG 2010. In February 2009, on the request of the Government of Delhi, the
Planning Commission approved inclusion of these projects under JNNURM
mainly for finance purposes. Since these were ongoing projects, there was no
evidence that DPRs for these projects were prepared and contained prescribed
details. The Department stated (October 2011) that as UIG projects were
approved at the time of CWG, prescribed procedures could not be followed.



Chapter 2 : Performance Audit

Prior sanction of SLSC for seeking assistance from Gol not

obtained

For seeking central assistance under JNNURM, projects are first to be
submitted to the SLSC by ULB through SLNA and then sent to CSMC.
However, 15 projects of BSUP and one project of UIG were submitted
directly to the CSMC by executing agencies/SLNA and after its approval,
these were submitted to SLSC, indicating it was bypassed in the project
formulation and seeking central assistance.

The Department stated (October 2011) that projects were submitted to the
CSMC in anticipation of approval of the SLSC.

6 Non-participation of ULB

The MCsD and the NDMC are the main parastatal agencies in Delhi,
however, no city development project was submitted by any of these agencies
under BSUP.

The UDD stated (October 2011) that DUSIB has taken approval for four
projects and the NDMC has submitted one proposal in BSUP. Reply is not
acceptable because these projects were housing projects only and not the city
development projects.

2.2.4.7 Non-setting up of Revolving Fund

UIG sub-mission provides that the SLNA should ensure that at least 25 per
cent of the funds released to the implementing agencies, are recovered and
ploughed into a Revolving Fund, maintained by it. This fund will be utilised
to leverage market funds for financing further investment in infrastructure
projects. At the end of the Mission period, the Revolving Fund may be
upgraded to a State Level Urban Infrastructure Fund.

Similarly, a revolving fund was envisaged under sub-mission for BSUP with
10 per cent of the funds released. At the end of the Mission period, the
Revolving Fund may be upgraded to a State level Basic Services to the Urban
Poor Fund. However, it was noticed that no revolving fund was set up by the
SLNA.

The Department confirmed (October 2011) the same.

2.2.4.8 Non-setting up of Programme Management Unit and

Programme Implementation Unit

With a view to strengthen the capacity of the SLNA to manage and implement
the composite array of tasks associated with JNNURM, a Programme
Management Unit (PMU) comprising specialists and experts, was designed in
the Mission. Similarly, a Programme Implementation Unit (PIU) was to be
established at ULB level. However, neither PMU nor PIU was set up by the
Government of NCT of Delhi.

53


Audit Report No. 2 of the year 2013

The Department stated (October 2011) that these units could not be set up but
these would be set up at the earliest. The reply is not convincing as the
mission has already been completed.

2.2.4.9 Deficient

The City Development Plan (CDP) was envisaged to be a comprehensive

document outlining the vision and development strategy for future

development of the city. It was to be prepared in consultation with

stakeholders to identify the thrust areas to be developed on priority basis. The

primary responsibility for preparing the CDP was on State and ULBs.

However, it was noticed that the Department appointed a private firm IL&FS

at a cost of हैं 18 lakh to prepare CDP.

The study of the CDP revealed that this document was not formulated in the

manner as described in the tool kit (guidelines issued by the Ministry of Urban

Employment and Poverty Alleviation and Ministry of Urban Development,

Gol). No critical analysis of the needs of the city was undertaken to prepare

this important document.

Scrutiny of CDP prepared by IL&FS revealed that it was merely a collection

of data and did not provide any vision and direction for future development of

the city. It did not mention:

> — strategy for bridging the gap between where the city is and where the
city wishes to go,

> — strategic options available to achieve the vision,

> aggregate investment needed to implement the plan, and

> further reforms that the city needs.

Thus, it was a deficient document.

2.2.4.10 Non- ui on of CDP as strategic document

DPRs submitted by the executing agencies clearly showed that no use of CDP
was made by any of the agencies and proposals submitted by them were
planned in isolation. No project suggested in CDP was implemented by any of
the ULBs. Thus, CDP was just a formality completed by the Government.

While confirming the facts, the Department stated that priority of projects was
rapidly changing in Delhi. Hence, the implementing agencies were submitting
the projects as per the current requirements, which might not be available in
CDP. The reply shows that the CDP was not used as a strategic document for
planning projects in Delhi. No mechanism was devised to keep it relevant in
future. It is evident that there is a complete disconnect between the long term
vision for Delhi and the proposal for current projects being executed.



Chapter 2 : Performance Audit

2.2.5 Monitoring and evaluation

2.2.5.1 Role of SLSC, SLNA, and ULBs

The SLSC or SLNA did not play any role in monitoring the JNNURM
projects being implemented in Delhi and the projects were solely in the hands
of the executing agencies. As a result, all UIG and BSUP projects were
lagging far behind the schedule. It was noticed that only six meetings of SLSC
were held during the entire mission period with a gap of five to 20 months
between two consecutive meetings, making the State level monitoring system
for the projects actually ineffective.

me Monitoring Evaluation System (PMES)

PMES was devised at the Gol level to keep a watch on the progress of
projects implemented in the States. Through this system, information on
progress of the projects was to be submitted on line by the SLNA. However,
the Department accepted that PMES did not exist in SLNA. This indicates that
very purpose of setting up of PMES 1.6. to submit the progress report to the
central government has been defeated and in absence of regular reporting by
the state government in prescribed manner, the central government was not in
a position to monitor the progress of the projects.

2.2.5.3 Non-appointment of Independent Review and Monitoring

Agencies

Independent Review and Monitoring Agencies (IRMA) were to be appointed
for each project to have a watch on physical and financial progress of the UIG
projects, Though 30 mega projects involving % 7878.48 crore were approved
by CSMC, the SLNA did not appoint IRMA for any of the projects. The
Department stated that since projects were ongoing under CWG Scheme, third
party quality audit was conducted in most of the projects, hence, IRMA was
not appointed. Reply is not acceptable as role of IRMA was far more
comprehensive than that of a third party quality consultant appointed by
implementing agencies.

2.2.5.4 Non/Delayed appointment of Third Party Inspection and

Monitoring Agency

Third Party Inspection and Monitoring Agency (TPIMA) was to be appointed
to keep a watch on the progress of BSUP projects. These agencies were to
inspect the site and submit their report to SLNA right from planning stage to
completion stage. Audit scrutiny revealed that CSMC entrusted 12 projects for
construction of 55120 flats between May 2007 and June 2008. However,
SLNA appointed TPIMA as late as in March 2011 at a fee of f 36.13 lakh. By
that time, four projects had been dropped, six had been completed or were
near completion, one could not be started due to stay order of the Court and

55


Audit Report No. 2 of the year 2013

only one project was in progress. Appointing the TPIMA for dropped and
completed projects has no relevance. It was also noticed that SLNA had not
appointed TIPMA for 12 projects approved between July 2008 and March
2012. Though the TIPMA has submitted 14 reports, SLNA had no
expert/specialist to get these reports examined and get the shortcomings
rectified.

The Department stated that TPIMA had submitted two reports each of seven
projects and that reports were being sent to implementing agencies to take
remedial action. The reply indicated that Department did not have an in-house
mechanism to examine the report of the TPIMA.

2.2.6 Financial management

CSMC approved 30 UIG projects and 24 BSUP projects, out of which five
UIG projects and six BSUP projects have been dropped. As of March 2012 an
expenditure of € 3335.80 crore had been incurred by implementing agencies.

2.2.6.1 No control of SLNA on Additional Central Assistance released

for UIG

As per guidelines, funds should be released as Additional Central Assistance
(ACA) to implementing agencies by SLNA. However, funds have been
released by the Finance Department or Urban Development Department
directly to the executing agencies without routing them through SLNA. In the
absence of the final accounts, proper utilization of the funds by the
implementing agencies could not be ensured. SLNA did not keep a track of
these funds. The Department stated (October 2011) that due to shortage of
time, funds were directly allotted to agencies by the Finance Department.

2.2.6.2 Non-release of Additional Central Assistance

Guidelines provide that first installment of 25 per cent of the committed
central assistance would be released on signing of the MoA by State
Government/parastatal agency. The balance amount would be released in
three installments upon receipt of Utilization Certificates and subject to
achievement of milestones. Gol committed to provide Additional Central
Assistance (ACA) of € 4588.56 crore for UIG projects and BSUP projects in
Delhi. But ACA of only F 1416.76 crore was received by the Government of
Delhi upto November 2012. No efforts by SLNA were made to get the
remaining ACA released by Gol.

2.2.6.3 Non-utilization of allocated funds

It was observed that against the allocation of f 2823.18 crore for UIG projects
by the Planning Commission, 30 projects for € 2732.20 crore only were
sanctioned, Out of these, five projects having sanctioned value of % 105.88
crore, have been dropped. Hence the SLNA could not utilize allocation of &



Chapter 2 : Performance Audit

196.86 crore. While confirming the facts, the Department stated (October
2011) that some more projects would be submitted to Gol to avail approved
ACA. However, no new project was submitted by Government of Delhi to
utilize remaining allocation as of till the closure of the scheme.

2.2.6.4 Infructuous expenditure of € 0.76 crore

Government of Delhi got approved a UIG project namely- ‘construction of
clover leaves at Karkari More and widening of existing bridge on trunk drain
at Karkardooma More’ and the work was entrusted to Delhi Development
Authority (DDA). It was observed that the project had been dropped due to
non-clearance by Unified Traffic and Transportation Infrastructure (Planning
& Engineering) Centre (UTTEPEC). However, DDA had already incurred an
expenditure of & 0.76 crore on this project which has become infructuous

2.2.6.5 Unwarranted release of रैं 200 crore to DUSIB

The Department released (December 2011) हैं 200 crore to DUSIB to enable it
to repay the loan to DSJIDC. However, it was noticed that DUSIB had no
liability to discharge against DSILDC for any loan. As such, the DUSIB did
not pay any amount to DSIIDC but kept the amount with it. Thus, the
Department had released € 200 crore to the DUSIB without any demand.
Reply of the Department was awaited as of November 2012.

2.2.6.6 Purchase of staff cars out of JNNURM funds

The DUSIB, an implementing agency for BSUP projects, had purchased eight
staff cars for ¥ 27.82 lakh out of the funds received by it for housing projects
under JNNURM.

2.2. Execution of the projects under BSUP

tency in the selection of projects taken up under BSUP

The main thrust of the sub-mission of BSUP was an integrated development
of slums through projects for providing shelter, basic services and other
related civil amenities with a view to provide utilities to the urban poor.

It was emphasized in 25" meeting of CSMC that there would be a limit of 50
to 60 per cent for housing projects and remaining for infrastructure projects
under JNNURM. However, it was noticed that the Government of Delhi had
undertaken only housing project- ‘Houses at affordable costs to slum
dwellers’. As estimated by DUSIB, about 47.36 lakh persons were residing in
about 4 lakh jhuggies scattered in 685 clusters in NCT of Delhi and in slum
designated areas. However, no programme for development of JJ clusters by
providing civic amenities or any project for old walled city development was
undertaken under JNNURM in Delhi.

(a) Execution of housing projects by 0577८

The DSIIDC was the largest implementing agency for housing projects under

projects. Shortcomings noticed during the audit are summarized below:

> In response to invitation of bids for construction of 5552 DUs at
Bapraulla, only one bid was received. The cost quoted by the only bidder
was almost double of the estimated cost put to tender. However, the
DSIIDC did not opt for retendering. When pointed out, DSIIDC stated
(January 2012) that they had adopted reasonability of rates in line with the
provisions of CPWD Works Manual. However, the DSIIDC did not reply
as to why option of retendering was not considered.

> The DSIIDC incurred % 50.59 lakh on projects which were shelved mainly
due to failure of the Government of NCT of Delhi to make available the
hindrance free land to DSIIDC for the housing projects.

> It was decided in the meeting of CSMC (December 2007) to keep the
stipulated period of completion of individual project below 18 months to
avoid payment for cost escalation. However, in four projects, the DSIIDC
paid हैं 12.97 crore to the contractor for cost escalation upto 31 March 2012
as stipulated time of completion was not kept below 18 months.

> Though 9660 DUs have been completed, approval for plan of construction
of these DUs was not taken by the DSIIDC from local bodies. The matter
was referred to the DSIIDC in June 2012 but reply was awaited as of

The CSMC sanctioned eight projects for 24076 DUs to be executed by the

Chapter 2 : Performance Audit

In two projects, against the approved cost of € 28.45 crore, the DUSIB

CSMC did not approve the project as there was increase in the cost.
Thus, inaction on the part of the DUSIB resulted in loss of opportunity
for 1200 DUs as sanction of new projects under JNNURM scheme has
since been closed.

Above facts show that the DUSIB did not complete even a single DU during
the mission period, inspite of incurring = 74.20 crore on various projects.

> The Government of NCT of Delhi declared (February 2010) the

DUSIB. However, in audit it was noticed that DUSIB had neither

> The DUSIB did not start the process to identify the urban poor, except

> The DUSIB had not yet decided as to how the funds received from
allottees and land owing agencies would be utilised.

The DUSIB stated (July 2012) that despite of shortage of manpower, expertise
and mechanism it had been making its best efforts to identify the beneficiaries
of the scheme. It further added that it had no mission to relocate all the JJ
clusters, instead it takes action only on the request of the land owning
agencies in the clusters prioritized by the department. From the reply, it is
clear that projects under the BSUP and work for allotment of DUs constructed
under the BSUP were assigned to the DUSIB by the Government of NCT of
Delhi without keeping in view the capacity and capability of the DUSIB.

Thus, the DTTDC revised the estimates six times between May 2005 and
October 2009 and took almost six years to award the work. The DTTDC
received AA & ES for % 887.29 crore (January 2008), but it took eight months
to call the tenders (August 2008) for main bridge, then three months to reject
the tender and another 3 months to submit the revised PE for हैं 1185.00 crore.
This situation could have been avoided had the DTTDC prepared and
submitted the PE on the basis of modified design of bridge at very first
occasion i.e. in November 2006 and then proceed for tendering process.

Delay in taking decision by the Government of NCT of Delhi also contributed
to the escalation in the estimated cost of the project as it rejected revised PE
for € 993.11 crore in May 2007 when quoted amount was % 845.97 crore only.
But, it had to approve the cost of € 1131.00 crore in February 2010 for the
same project. Thus, failure of the DTTDC to manage the events increased the
estimated cost of the project from हैं 459.00 crore in March 2006 to हैं 1131.00
crore in February 2010 (an increase of € 672 crore). Moreover, a project
planned to be completed in August 2008, has now been rescheduled to be
completed in December 2013.

The DTIDC in its reply stated (January 2012) that cost escalation was
predominantly due to increase in price of material and foreign exchange.
Reply is not acceptable as cost escalation was due to failure of the DITDC to
manage the events timely and effectively.

() Violation of terms and conditions of agreement

The fabrication and assembling of the structural steel for deck and pylon was
to be carried out in any of the three Indian fabrication shops specified in the
agreement or in equivalent shops approved by Engineer-in-charge. However,
it was noticed that:

(a) the contractor was getting fabrication work done in China without
formal approval of the Engineer-in-charge in violation of terms and
conditions of agreement,

(b) in the agreement, there was another condition to use structural steel
manufactured by any of the four shortlisted Indian firms. However, the
steel was being procured from China for deck and pylon, and

(०) it was the primary responsibility of the DITDC to ensure quality of
the material being used in the bridge. This aspect was not ensured as
the DTTDC had no mechanism of its own to ensure quality of material
and work being done in China.

The DITDC stated (January 2012) that audit had inferred wrongly that only
Indian workshops were to be engaged for fabrication work and added that for
quality aspect a firm had been deployed by contractor to check quality of the
material and fabrication work in China. The said firm reports directly to the
DTTDC. The DITDC further added (July 2012) that contractor had not

63


Audit Report No. 2 of the year 2013

proposed any firm shortlisted in NIT. The formal communication was to be
issued by the structural consultant.

The reply is not acceptable as the names of the approved fabricator firms were
clearly mentioned in NIT and agreement and all these were Indian firms.
From the reply of DTTDC (20 July 2012), it is clear that contractor has
proposed the name of Chinese firm and that was accepted by consultant who
was a private entity. As quality control firm was appointed by contractor
himself, it may not function as an independent agency of the DTTDC. The
reply was silent on the issue of purchase of steel from China.

(d) Unauthorised and irregular release of € 20.60 crore to contractor

The DITDC released हैं 20.60 crore to contractor upto May 2012 as secured
advance for the material which was procured and stored in China, in violation
of provisions of CPWD Works Manual" for payment of secured advance.
Audit observed that:

(i) the material was not brought at construction site by the agency,

(ii) the DTTDC has not certified the procurement, availability, quantity,
necessity and cost and specification of the material procured in China,
and

(iii) the required agreement securing lien over material was also not
entered into with the contractor.

The DITDC stated (August 2012) that payment to the contractor was in
accordance with the clause of agreement. Reply is not acceptable as
provisions of Manual to grant secured advance applies to all types of contract.

(e) Award of work of 5.99 crore to consultant without completing codal
formalities

(0). #एणएा the appointment of consultant for main bridge, DITDC sent
(October 2005) its offer only to M/s Schlaich Bergermann Und Partner
(SBP) Germany, requesting them to submit their financial and technical
bids. DITTDC awarded (December 2005) the work to the firm at हैं 5.99
crore. Award of work without inviting tenders was against extant rules.
The department stated (August 2012) that as SBP was well conversant
with Indian environment and were the main designer of a bridge in
Kolkata, consultation work was given to them. Reply is not acceptable
as awarding the work without tendering was not justified.

(ii) It was also noticed that the DITDC allowed the SBP to form a joint
venture with Construma Consultancy Pvt. Ltd, Mumbai (CCPL). As per
the bid documents and MoU signed between the SBP and the CCPL, the
SBP was to work as lead consultant and overview the work of sub-

However, it was noticed that drawing and design of all components

were not verified and without ensuring fair market competition.

(6). Irregular and undue payment to contractor of % 4 crore in name of
price variation

As per the agreement, clause 10 CA of Works Manual was applicable for
payment due to variation in price of material. Payment under 10 CA depends
upon the base price for cement and steel, issued by Director General (Works),
CPWD, on the basis of price indices decided by Gol from time to time.
However, it was noticed that the DTTDC paid %4 crore for the material
procured by the contractor in China on the basis of price index decided by DG
(Works). As the indices determined by the Director General (Works) CPWD
or by Gol are not applicable in China, the payment of = 4 crore for price
variation was irregular.

DTTDC stated (August 2012) that the materials might be procured from
anywhere and it is not limited to place of construction only. The different
materials might be procured from different places. Since the construction site
is in Delhi, the base prices and price indices issued under the authority of
Director General (Works), CPWD, applicable for Delhi, have been taken in
Schedule-F of the agreement. Reply is not acceptable because as per schedule-
F of the agreement, the material was to be procured from the firms mentioned
in the agreement and price was based on the price index prevailing in Delhi
only.

to main bridge

(a) Award of contract without inviting open tenders

Audit observed that for the work of construction of ‘Approaches’, the DITDC
invited bids only from 10 agencies, short listed by the PWD for works having
cost of $100 crore and above. The DTTDC added one additional condition in

construction. It was despite the fact that PWD invited tenders without such



Audit Report No. 2 of the year 2013

condition for its own work — ‘Elevated Corridor flyover at Naraina’ with pre

the justified cost and &74 crore more than the estimated cost, both worked out
by the DITDC after allowing 37.5 percent as contractor’s profit and

66


Chapter 2 : Performance Audit

(a) Extra expenditure of 770 lakh in construction of road

Section 2702.1.2 of MORTH Specifications for Road and Bridges which
provides only 50mm thick DBC as wearing coat. Deck of the flyover was
made of pre-cast RCC slab placed on steel girders. The wearing course of the
deck as per approved drawing was to be constructed by applying water proof
membrane and 50 mm thick bituminous concrete (BC). However, it was
noticed that the DITDC has provided base course of 75 mm thick Dense
Bituminous Macadam (DBM) on RCC deck slab followed by one layer of 50
mm thick Dense Bituminous Concrete (DBC) as wearing course on water
proof membrane. Thus, over design of pavement of flyover resulted in extra
expenditure of € 70 lakh on DBM.

DTTDC stated (January 2012) that the exact slope/level/curvature at every
point, was not possible with BC only and so DBM coarse was provided for
profile correction. Reply is not acceptable as the required slope/camber was
provided in the steel girders as per approved drawings. Moreover, neither
changes in drawing nor extra item for providing DBM was approved by the
competent authority.

(b) Unauthorized expenditure of F 69.72 lakh

As per general provisions of MoU, cost of consultancy for the work was to be
borne by the DITDC, out of departmental charges. However, it was noticed
that the DTTDC made a payment of हैं 69.72 lakh up to May 2012 for
architectural consultancy and charged the amount to the expenditure sanction
for the main work. This was in violation of MoU and resulted in unauthorized
expenditure of € 69.72 lakh.

The DTTDC stated (January 2012) that there was a provision for consultancy
work in AA & ES. Reply is not acceptable as MoU was signed after AA & ES
was approved.

UTTIPEC® had been intimated regarding these changes.

The reply is not acceptable as the PWD should have obtained the approval of
the Government for reduction in the scope of work.

for payment for cost escalation for this period also.

Manager F-11 after detailed scrutiny of hindrances and he was fully

be made signal free by providing grade separator at this crossing. Flyover on
G.T. Road, under pass on Road No.62-56, surface rotary for right turning
movement, slip road and three FOBs were planned under the project.

(a) Payment of € 1 crore due to injudicious decision

There was a provision of 248 number of soil anchors (agreement rate हैं. 1.25
lakh per piece) for the C/o Underpass at Apsara Boarder. However, a total of
761 soil anchors were used but no justification for three fold increase in
quantity was recorded. As per Schedule-F of Clause 12.2 of the Agreement,
deviation in quantity upto 30 per cent for sub-structure and 100 per cent for
foundation item was to be executed at agreement rate. The PWD had paid for
457 anchors at the agreement rates and for 304 anchors at market rates. The
market rate worked out by the PWD for one soil anchor was हैं 50028, which is
lesser than the agreement rate by हैं 74972, It was noticed that soil anchor was
not considered as a foundation item upto 25" RA Bill. But on the demand of
the contractor, the department treated this item partly as foundation item and
partly as non-foundation item in 267 RA Bill without giving reasons for the
same. As soil anchors were not foundation item, contractor was entitled to be
paid for 323 anchors only at agreement rate and remaining 438 anchors at
market rate. Thus, injudicious decision of the department to pay for 457
anchors against the eligible 323 anchors at the agreement rate, resulted in
extra payment off | crore to the contractor.



Audit Report No. 2 of the year 2013

The PWD stated (January 2012) that the underpass does have foundation like
other structure and the same is below ground and was legitimately categorized
as superstructure. The reply is not acceptable as whole of the underpass was
constructed below the ground level and soil anchors by the definition under
Clause-F, does not qualify to be a foundation item.

(b) Non levy of penalty of € 18 crore for delay

The stipulated date of completion of the project was 9 June 2010 but the work
was actually completed on 22 November 2011 with a delay of 532 days. The
Project Manager granted EOT for 532 days without levy of penalty.
However, scrutiny of record revealed that EOT of 104 days was allowed
without levy of penalty for non availability of structural drawings for FOBs. It
was not justified, as the construction of FOBs was an independent activity and
not interlinked with main work. The work of main flyover was not required to
be kept in abeyance till completion of FOBs. Moreover, reference for non-
availability of drawings was not mentioned in hindrance register. Similiarly,
EOT of 128 days was allowed without levy of penalty for extra work. It is
also not justified, as extra works were to be executed simultaneously with
other works and in that case overlapping period should have been deducted
from 128 days.

Thus, a penalty of f 18 crore for delay of 232 days should have been levied on
contractor.

The PWD stated (July 2012) that 104 days were considered for non-
availability of structure drawing for FOBs and the consultant who was
responsible for delay had already been penalized. The period for extra work
has been considered in granting of EOT as per CPWD Works Manual. Reply
is not acceptable as PWD did not specify the reasons for not carrying out the
work for the underpass and flyover, in absence of drawings for FOB and not
considering the overlapping time on account of extra items

(a) Loss of € 1.54 crore due to inconsistent clause in agreement

A test check of agreements/NITs for all the five works revealed that the
PWD/DTTDC introduced a clause 10CCA for price variation. Price variation
is paid or recovered only on consumable items. The CPWD Manual does not
prescribe any payment on account of variation in price of machinery and
machine tools (capital goods). However, under clause 10 CCA, PWD
prescribed eight to 18 per cent (eight per cent -IIT Gate, 18 per cent Apsara
Border) component of total work for paying price variation on ‘machinery and
machine tools’, resulting in extra expenditure of हैं 1.54 crore.

The PWD stated (January 2012) that the provision to compensate the
contractor for tools and machinery against price escalation was kept with the
approval of the competent authority. The fact remains that the introduction of

70


Chapter 2 : Performance Audit

the clause in the agreement, though with the approval of the competent
authority, had resulted in extra expenditure of 1.54 crore.

Audit Report No. 2 of the year 2013

Conclusions and Recommendations

Conclusion

Implementation of the Mission by the Government of NCT of Delhi was not
entirely satisfactory. The implementation of mandatory and optional reforms
in Delhi needs to be taken further and articulated by the Delhi Government to
meet its development objectives. The State Level Nodal Agency (Urban
Development Department) was practically ineffective as it had not equipped
itself with the qualified manpower or technology to oversee and monitor the
projects. As a consequence, the mechanism for monitoring the projects under
the Mission was not in place. Although, the UIG projects by PWD have been
largely completed, other UIG projects undertaken by 01170, MCD and DJB
were lagging behind badly. Though, the main thrust of the BSUP was on
integrated development of slums through projects for providing shelter, basic
services and other related civic amenities, only a few projects of housing for
slum dwellers had been taken up. Even, the dwelling units constructed under
the BSUP were not allotted for want of verification of eligible beneficiaries,
as survey of the urban poor is yet to be started by the DUSIB. Execution of
projects was marred by irregularities resulting in cost over run and extra
burden on public exchequer.

Recommendations

The Mission is in the extended period only for completion of ongoing and
sanctioned projects. Since the extended period is upto 2014, for timely
completion of pending projects, the Government may consider taking the
following actions:

> SLNA may strengthen its monitoring mechanism with qualified staff
and technology to have a constant watch on the progress of
ongoing projects.

> The Government may step up its efforts to complete left over
projects within the extended period of the Mission.

> The DUSIB may start the survey of urban poor and ensure early
allotment of completed dwelling units to the identified eligible
beneficiaries.

> The SLNA (Urban Development Department) may obtain UCs from
the implementing agencies and submit them further to the Gol, to
claim the balance funds sanctioned under the Mission.

> Implementing agencies may be advised to submit the final accounts
of the projects executed by them

76


3.1 Thematic Audit of Water M

Executive Summary

A thematic audit of the Delhi Jal Board was conducted with a view to
ascertaining the deficiencies in supply of potable water to the population of
Delhi. The raw water available in Delhi at present is not sufficient to provide
potable water to the whole population of Delhi as per prescribed norms. Two
dams were proposed on river Yamuna in 1994 to augment raw water in Delhi,
but were not constructed even after a lapse of 18 years after incurring an
expenditure of 214 crore. The production of potable water at WTPs and
waste water recycling plants was also found to be below designed capacity.
The 8-MGD recycling plant at Chandrawal was commissioned after a delay of
more than four years. Due to the absence of proper measurement system,
whether the wastage of water during treatment process was within permissible
limits at Chandrawal could not be ascertained.

The allocation of jurisdictional areas among divisions was based on Assembly
Constituencies and not command areas of different treatment plants, due to
which, proper measurement of water supplied to each division was not
possible and accountability for loss of water could not be fixed. The drinking
water was not being distributed equitably amongst the population due to lack
of reliable data on population and water supplied to different areas. Further,
24.8 per cent of the households in Delhi were being supplied water through
tankers in the absence of pipe lines.The average per capita supply was 3.82
litres per day against a prescribed norm of 172 litres. Metering of water at
consumer end was not comprehensive due to which, less than 40 per cent of
water produced was billed during last three years.



Audit Report No. 2 of the year 2013

3.1.1 Introduction

The Delhi Jal Board (DJB), constituted under the Delhi Water Board Act,
1998, is responsible for the supply and distribution of potable water in the
area under the jurisdiction of the Municipal Corporation of Delhi. The DJB
also supplies water in bulk to New Delhi Municipal Council and Delhi
Cantonment Board. The sources of water for producing potable water are
YamunaRiver, Ganga water from Uttar Pradesh, water from Bhakra through
Haryana, Ranney wells and tube wells.

The DJB has nine water treatment plants (WTPs). Six plants are functional
with a combined capacity of production of 690 million gallons daily (MGD)
of potable water. Apart from this, there are four process waste water recycling
plants (total capacity 45 MGD) which produces potable water from waste
water of WTP.

3.1.1.1 Organizational set up

The DJB functions under the Chairmanship of the Chief Minister of Delhi and
is assisted by a Vice Chairperson, nominated by the Speaker of the Legislature
and 16 other members consisting of 10 political/ex-officio members and six
administrative/executive members. Member (Water) heads the Engineering
Wing for water activities which is under the overall control of Chief
Executive Officer (CEO), who is assisted by Chief Engineers, Superintending
Engineers and Executive Engineers.

3.1.2 Audit scope and methodology

The thematic audit was conducted from June to November 2012, covering the
period from April 2009 to September 2012. Records at DJB Headquarters,
WTPs, Booster Pumping Stations, Process Waste Water Recycling Plants,
Civil and Electrical and Mechanical (E&M)Divisions, Treatment and Quality
Control Wing at Wazirabad and Revenue Wing at the DJB headquarters were
examined in audit.An entry conference was held in July 2012 with the DJB to
discuss the objectives and scope of audit.

All the 22 civil divisions and nine E&M divisions of the DJB were covered
for collection of data. Two out of the six operational WTPs (Chandrawal and
Wazirabad) and three ProcessWaste Water Recycling Plants were test checked
(Chandrawal, Haiderpur and Wazirabad). A detailed study of the water trails
emanating from two WIPs, viz. Chandrawal and Nangloi, their related
reservoirs, command areas and distribution lines was conducted

With a view to assessing public perception about water shortage situation,a
questionnaire was sent to randomly selected 584 RWAs out of 1947 registered
under the Bhagidari scheme of the Government of NCT of Delhi (GNCTD).
Responses were received from 111 RWAs.

An exit conference was held on 06 March 2013 to discuss the audit findings
and draw the views of the DJB on the same.

80


Chapter 3 : Thematic Audit

3.1.2.1 Audit objective

The objective of thematic audit was to ascertain whether:

> the raw water available in Delhi was sufficient to provide adequate
drinking water to whole of the population in Delhi,

> available raw water was being processed in WTPs in an efficient manner
with minimum wastage,

>» quality of drinking water produced and supplied conformed to the
prescribed standards,

» the system for distribution of water was designed for efficient utilisation
of available water and to ensure supply of water to whole of the
population as per prescribed norms,

> the system of billing and collection was efficient, and
> complaint redressal system was effective.

Audit findings

Augmentation of raw water availabil

As per provisional figures of Census 2011, the population of Delhi in 2011
was 167.53 lakh, growing at the rate of 1.92per cent per annum. Accordingly,
the projected population of Delhi in 2012 is 170.75 lakh and it would be
202.64 lakh in 2021 Considering per capita requirement of 60 gallons per day,
current requirement of potable water in Delhi works out to 1025 MGD and for
2021,it would be 1216 MGD. Against this, the current production is only
about 818 MGD due to shortage of raw water, leaving a gap of 207 MGD.

In terms of a Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) signed in May 1994 by
five basin States, namely Delhi, Haryana, Uttar Pradesh, Rajasthan and
Himachal Pradesh, the tentative allocation of Yamuna surface water to Delhi
was 0.724 Billion Cubic Metres (BCM) per year. The seasonal allocation of
this water is given in Table 3.1.

Table 3.1: Allocation of Yamuna water to Delhi

Period Allocation (in BCM)
| 1 | July to October (monsoon season) 0.580
2 | November to February 0.068

Total 0.724

As can be seen from the above, 80 per cent of the allocation is during
monsoon season,when most of the water flows through Delhi untapped. The
allocation during the remaining two seasons works out to approximately

81


Audit Report No. 2 of the year 2013

20 per cent. In order to utilise flow of the river during monsoon, MoU was
signed (November 1994) by the basin States to build two dams up the river at
Renuka (275 MGD) in Himachal Pradesh and Kishau (372 MGD) in Uttar
Pradesh (now in Uttarakhand).The GNCTD had released (October 2008) an
amount of % 214 crore to the Government of Himachal Pradesh for
construction of Renuka dam for exclusive use of Delhi. However, work on
these dams had not started even after a lapse of more than 18 years due to
disagreement between the beneficiary States regarding sharing of waters and
hydro power. Construction of Renuka dam is pending for forest clearance
from the Ministry of Environment and Forest, Gol as the clearance earlier
accorded was challenged in the Green Tribunal. Kishau dam in Uttarakhand
was yet to be taken up.

The raw water for Haiderpur plant is released by the Government of Haryana
at Munak (in Haryana) into Delhi branch channel and it flows to Haiderpur
plant. In order to reduce loss of water during transit, Haryana proposed
(February 1990) construction of an exclusive parallel Water Carrier System
(WCS). The WCS was to be utilised for carrying raw water to Wazirabad
Water Treatment Plant also. Accordingly, an MoU was signed (February
1993) between Haryana and Delhi for construction of this WCS at an
estimated cost of € 314 crore. Haryana was to maintain and operate this WCS
for which annual charges were to be paid by Delhi. Once operational, a saving
of 80 MGD of water was expected which would have been sufficient to run
three WTPs, one each at Dwarka, Bawana and Okhla. The construction was
almost complete in June 2012 and by that time Delhi government had released
%414 crore. Later, a dispute arose between Delhi and Haryana regarding
ownership of the expected savings of 80 MGD of raw water. Delhi
Government approached (June 2012) Central Government for resolution of
the matter. However, the expenditure of € 414 crore on construction of the
WCS, remained unfruitful as Delhi did not receive the additional quantity of
raw water of 80 MGD

It was observed that any significant step towards augmentation of the
availability of raw water would take about 6-7 years to realize and by that
time the population of Delhi would have increased and so would the demand
for water.

The DJB has six operational WTPs with acombined capacity of 690 MGD of

Audit examined records of two WTPs (Chandrawal and Wazirabad) in detail
and audit findings are discussed in succeeding paragraphs.

(a) Absence of measurement of wastage at Chandrawal WIP

Raw water for Chandrawal WTP is drawn from Yamuna at Wazirabad. Prior
to construction of a twin pipeline from Wazirabad to Chandrawal in April
2012, raw water was supplied through open channels and there was no reliable
data on receipt of raw water at Chandrawal. Thereafter, actual reading of raw
water intake could commence only in July 2012, as one of the flow meters
was out of order.

Similarly, measurement of potable water produced at Chandrawal was also
not found reliable. Two methods of calculations were found recorded in
the log sheets maintained at ChandrawalWTP-I, the first one was based on
flow meter recordings and the other one on the rated capacity of the pumps
used. It was seen during test check that the reading recorded for 24 hours on
26 July 2012 and 28 July 2012, by the flow meter was 36 MGD (on both
days), while as per traditional method, water pumped out was 32 and 34.22
MGBD respectively. Flow meters are considered to provide accurate and actual
measurement of water that flowed through the pipeline, whereas reading
based on rate of pumping tends to be higher than actual as pumps do not
function technically at their 100 per cent efficiency. However, in this case,
flow meter reading was more than that calculated on the basis of rate of
pumping. On enquiry, the EE (E&M-I), stated (November 2012) that
repeated complaints were placed with E&M (HP II) about the unrealistic and
erratic readings shown by flow meters at both the works.

In case of Chandrawal-ll, log sheets for potable water pumped out from the
plant was maintained on the basis of capacity of pumps used and the duration
of pumping. Since the pumps were very old and were functioning with
decreased efficiency, the quantity of water worked out in the log sheets was
not accurate.

83


Audit Report No. 2 of the year 2013

In the absence of accurate information regarding raw water intake and potable
water produced, audit could not derive assurance that the Chandrawal WTP
was working efficiently and the wastage of water during purification process
was within the permissible limits of eight to ten per cent.

The DJB stated (March 2013) that the flow meters are working properly now
and the wastage during production is about eight per cent. However, Audit
could not verify that the wastage was within permissible limits as there was no
system for measuring wastage during treatment at the time of audit.

(b) Wastage of potable water due to defective valves and sluice gates

During visit to Chandrawal WTP-IIon 18 to 21 June 2012 (three days), it was
seen that the valves installed in the pipelines carrying potable water into the
UGR from the filters were leaking continuously.

It was stated (July 2012) by the Executive Engineer that the valves and sluice
gates on the filter beds of the plant were leaking as they were more than 57
years old and remained unrepaired/un-replaced due to budget constraints. Test
check of log sheets revealed that the operational staff had recorded the
necessity of immediate stoppage of major leakages in the sluice valves which
also hampered proper cleaning of filters as the required pressure of potable
water was not available. Inaction on the part of DJB in plugging the leakages
indicates insensitivity towards wastage of precious potable water.

The DJB replied (March 2013) that replacing the sluice valves and gates
without a holistic approach towards maintenance and rehabilitation plan is not
advisable as it can result in disruption of services and therefore, the existing
system is being maintained by incurring bare minimum expenditure. A
complete rehabilitation of Chandrawal WTP and its command area is
expected to be taken up by 2015. However, allowing leakage of precious
potable water to continue, expecting it to be plugged during rehabilitation to
be taken up in 2015 does not appear prudent.

(c) Non-removal of silt from River Yamuna at Wazirabad pond

The pond on Yamuna River bed is the source of raw water for Wazirabad and
Chandrawal WTPs. Bhagirathi and Sonia Vihar WTPs also draw raw water
from Wazirabad during closure for annual cleaning of Murad Nagar conduits
which bring Ganga water from Uttar Pradesh to these plants. Records
revealed that the deposit of silt reduces the capacity of pond as depth of water
is reduced by three to four feet. Dredging of the pond was last carried out in
July 2006. Although a proposal was mooted for de-silting by Executive
Engineer (E&M-II) in September 2011, only administrative approval for the
same was given by the Board and tender was yet to be called for as of January
2013. Delay in dredging resulted in reduction in capacity of the pond thereby
depriving the DJB of any water reserve to meet emergency situations

In its reply (March 2013), the DJB stated that since dredging is more
prevalent in coastal areas, not much expertise exists in Delhi due to which it

84


Chapter 3 : Thematic Audit

took time to develop clarity on methodology and conditions to define scope of
work. The reply is not convincing as dredging was carried out earlier also in
the year 2006.

(d) Underutilisation of capacity of pumps

As per Energy Audit Report (December 2006) for WIP-I, Chandrawal, the
operating capacity of two out of 10 pumps used for pumping potable water,
were 57 percent and 63.7 percent respectively, which were below the
designed efficiency of 85 percent. The reasons for lower output were- (i)
smaller size of delivery pipe due to which velocity of water in these pumps
were more than acceptable limits and (ii) operation of the pumps at a higher
head than the rated figure. The Energy Auditors recommended modification
of delivery pipelines, but no action was taken by the DJB as of October 2012
resulting in underutilisation of the pumps.

The DJB replied (March 2013) that once the rehabilitation of Chandrawal
WTP is complete, the issue will be resolved. The reply is not convincing as
even after six years, no action was taken on the report that was prepared in
2006.

ing plants

the form of sludge from clarifiers and backwash from filters (water used to
wash the filters). The average permissible wastage of water is eight to ten per
cent. In order to augment the production of potable water, the DJB
constructed four Process Waste Water Recycling Plants (PWWRP)as detailed

(a) Delay in commissioning of recycling plant at Chandrawal
DJB entered into an agreement (February 2007) with M/S Gannon

Dunkerley& Co. Ltd. for construction of PWWRP with a capacity to produce
eight MGD of raw water on turnkey basis at a cost of Rs 12.86 crore, with

85


Audit Report No. 2 of the year 2013

stipulated date of commissioning as 30 June 2008 including three months trial
run. The raw water produced by this plant was to be fed to the inlet of the
main WTP.

The recycling plant was stated to be functional from 28 September 2012 and
has been producing raw water since then. However, scrutiny of records
revealed that the plant was producing only 4.70 MGD of raw water on an
average during the period from September to October 2012 against its
designed capacity of eight MGD. The plant was yet to be commissioned as of
November 2012.

Thus, delay in commissioning of the recycling plant deprived the people of
more than seven MGD of precious potable water for more than four years,
considering wastage of eight per cent during treatment.

The DJB replied (March 2013) that the delay in commissioning was due to
various reasons like extreme site constraints and high ground water table
which impeded construction of deep chambers, demolition and reconstruction
of chambers necessitated by insufficient space, hindrance in deep excavation
due to other underground pipes etc. It was also stated that the plant is now
fully commissioned. However, the fact remains that the plant was
commissioned after a delay of more than four years. The site constraints and
other difficulties could have been foreseen.

(b) Low production of water due to damaged waste water pipelines

The recycling plant at Wazirabad was required to produce 11 MGD of potable
water after collecting waste water from the three 40 MGD plants. The plant
was commissioned i December 2009. The pipeline carrying waste water
from one of the plants was damaged in January 2010 and thereafter, waste
water from only the remaining two WTPs was being utilised for production of
raw water. Due to this, the average production of water by the recycling plant
during January-June 2012 was reduced to 7.34 MGD against full capacity of
11 MGD. Though two years have passed, damaged pipe has not been repaired
which resulted in short production of potable water to the extent of 3.66
MGD.

The DJB, in its reply (March 2013), stated that the pipe line bringing waste
water from one of the plants crossed another pipeline for supply of water from
Haiderpur to Wazirabad and vice-versa in emergency. Therefore, waste water
pipeline required re-routing under the other pipeline and the work is likely to
be completed within next two months. However, the reply did not explain
why repairs/re-routing of a pipeline damaged in January 2010 was taking such
a long time.

(c) Short production at recycling plant, Haiderpur

Two WTPs atHaiderpur of 100 MGD capacity each collectively produce
about 16 MGD of waste water, for which a recycling plant of 16 MGD

capacity has been constructed by M/s Larson and Toubro Ltd., who was also
entrusted with the operation and maintenance of the plant. The plant is in two
parts. The first part converts the waste water into raw water and the second
produces potable water from this raw water. As per the contract, 97 per cent
of the waste water was to be converted into potable water.

Audit scrutiny of data of water produced during 12 months for the period from

that during this period, the plant produced 4788 MG of potable water from

cent. The short production of potable water due to excessive wastage worked
out to 1.4 MGD.

The DJB stated (March 2013) that the recycling plant is required to be
operated at the peak efficiency for nine months in a year since during
monsoon season, the efficiency will reduce due to increase in turbidity in the
raw water which affect recovery of water at the recycling plant. The reply is
not correct since the plant was not giving the desired output in any of the
months for which data was provided to Audit.

The DJB has water testing laboratories at all WTPs, beside six Zonal
laboratories to ensure quality of potable water as per BIS norms. The
laboratories conduct chemical and bacteriological analysis of water from
WTPs, reservoirs, samples ftom private bodies/individuals, tube wells, Deep
Bore Hand Pumps, Ranneywells and other sources.As part of thematic audit,
records of Quality Control Laboratory, Wazirabad was examined. Audit
findings are discussed in the succeeding paragraphs.

(a) Shortage of manpower

As per information made available to audit, against sanctioned strength of 180
for quality control, only 118 regular staffs beside 16 technicians on contract
basis, were available leaving a shortage of 46 staff.

(b) No corrective measures on deficiencies reported in test reports

In addition to its own laboratories, DJB has also engaged the services of
National Environmental Engineering Research Institute (NEERI), a
government agency, to conduct third party quality checks of potable water.
Corrective measures are to be taken by Zonal Engineers on deficiencies
pointed out in reports of field laboratories and NEERI. Audit observed delays
in taking remedial action by Zonal Engineers on reports forwarded by the
Field and Zonal Laboratories and NEERI indicating absence of an effective
internal control mechanism within the DJB.Out of 19 cases pertaining to the
period from July 2011 to September 2011 test checked, in five cases, no

87


Audit Report No. 2 of the year 2013

remedial action was found to have been taken by the Zonal offices on
rechecking. As a result, substandard potable water was used by the public.

The DJB replied (March 2013) that since the city has intermittent water
supply system, period of physical checking and collection of water samples
gets restricted. Due to these reasons, corrective measures to restore water
supply becomes time consuming. Steps are being taken to improve upon it.

3.1.4 Distribution of potable water

With a per capita requirement of 60 gallons (274 litres) of water per day
including domestic, institutional, industrial and fire service requirement, the
total requirement of potable water for Delhi works out to 1025 MGD, against
which the DJB produces and distributes about 818 MGD of potable water, the
shortage being 20.02 per cent. Nevertheless, the management of distribution
of water by DJB was found to be deficient on various counts as discussed in
the succeeding paragraphs.

3.1.4.1 Irrational assignment of jurisdictional areas to Divisions for

supplying potable water

In Delhi, six WTPs cater to the water need of the population of individual
command areas. Potable water is initially pumped through transmission lines
into Underground Reservoirs (UGRs) located in command areas, from where
it is further pumped into smaller UGRs and then distributed to households
through distribution lines with the help of booster pumps. Pumping of water
into various UGRs and distribution lines is managed by nine E&M divisions
of the DJB whereas distribution lines are maintained by 22 civil divisions.
Audit observed that the allocation of jurisdictional areas amongst various
divisions, E&M as well as Civil, was done on the basis of Assembly
Constituencies. Consequently, each division has to cater to two or more
constituencies. In this set up, some E&M divisions receive water from two or
more WTPs and most of them supply water to more than one civil division
making it difficult to track the flow of water from WTPs to the users.
Similarly, individual civil division get water supply from more than one E&M
division. In some cases, water from one UGR is supplied to areas under more
than one civil division. Some time, a single distribution line covers parts of
two civil divisions also. In this scenario, water supplied to each division is
not measurable, making proper management and distribution of available
potable water ineffective and inefficient respectively. Due to the complicated
system of distribution, water is not supplied to divisions according to the
population of the area under their jurisdiction. As such, the present allocation
of distribution work amongst various divisions is not conducive to efficient
water management and equitable distribution of potable water to the
population of Delhi. A proper distribution of jurisdictional areas will also aid
in making individual civil division accountable for water received, distributed,
distribution loss, non-revenue water, etc.

88


Chapter 3 : Thematic Audit

In reply, the DJB stated (March 2013) that the management deficiencies were
largely because the city had been growing partly in an unplanned manner. It
was further stated that the concept of district metering area was being
implemented through which it would become possible to account for the water
supplied and consumed in the command of specific water facility. It would
also help in distributing water more evenly and reduce non-revenue water.

Planning process of DJB should have taken into account the population
growth patterns and expansion of the city. Further, as regards implementation
of the concept of district metering area, the DJB has not given any time frame
for the same.

3.1.4.2 Population not receiving piped water

As per information available from census 2011, 24.8 per cent of the
households in Delhi were not receiving piped treated water. As per
information furnished by 15 out of 22 civil divisions, a population of 32.53
lakh not covered through pipelines, was supplied with 1000.94 MG of water
through tankers during the year 2011-12 with average per capita supply of
3.82 litres per day against a prescribed norm of 172 litres per capita per day
for domestic use. The details are in Annexure-3.1.

The DJB stated (March 2013) that tanker supply is an emergency supply to
meet drinking water needs only and for non-potable purposes, water demand
is met through ground water resources in the areas. However, the fact remains
that DJB failed to supply adequate piped treated water to 24.8 per cent of
households in Delhi.

3.1.4.3 Short supply/non-availability of water in areas at tail end of

distribution lines

Audit observed that DJB was also supplying water through tankers to areas
connected by distribution lines. The reason for this was shortage of water in
those areas due to installation of online booster pumps by consumers. As per
information furnished by 11 civil divisions, more than seven lakh people were
affected due to shortage of water in areas covered through distribution lines.
On this issue, DJB stated that although installation of on line booster pumps
by consumers is not permitted, action is taken only when a specific complaint
is received. DJB’s approach towards removal of illegal online boosters was
not proactive, even though this was affecting proper distribution of potable
water.

In reply (March 2013), the DJB stated that three magistrates had been
appointed for taking action against those who indulge in installation of online
boosters and illegal tappings but this drive needed to be pursued more to
address the problem of shortage of water in tail end areas. However, the reply
is silent about concrete action plan and time frame for addressing this issue.

89


Audit Report No. 2 of the year 2013

3.1.4.4 Non-accountabilit water supplied

The distribution of potable water is the responsibility of civil divisions which
maintain distribution lines and attend to consumer grievances, complaints
relating to short supply, unauthorized connections, installation of meters etc.
However, there is no system in DJB to measure the quantity of water received
by each division and actually distributed to consumers. Due to this, DJB is
not in a position to determine accountability with regard to wastages,
leakages, non-revenue water etc.

Although a project for installation of 305 flow meters was launched in July
2007 with scheduled date of completion as 18 July 2009, only 284 meters
were installed till May 2012. Further, information from these flow meters is
not of much use as it does not give quantity of water supplied to individual
divisions.

The DJB stated (March 2013) that once the entire network is divided into
district metering areas, it would be possible to account for the water supplied
and consumed. THowever, the DJB has not given any specific details of
schedule of implementation.

Water trail from Nangloi WTP

The water trail from Nangloi WTP was analysed in detail in audit to illustrate
the deficiencies in distribution system. The Nangloi WTP produces
approximately 40 MGD of water and supplies to areas under four civil
divisions through two E&M Divisions. Water from the WTP is also being
supplied to IGI Airport and to DDA.

There are two transmission lines emanating from the WTP, one towards
Pappankalan and the other towards Najafgarh. The Pappankalan line supplies
water mostly to areas under West III division through three primary UGRs at
Hastal village, Bodella and Panchwati. Apart from these, there is one more
UGR, Command Tank-2 (CT-2) being maintained by the DDA served by this
line. Seven distribution lines also branch out from the transmission lines
which supply water directly to Vikas Nagar, Mundka, Nagloi, Dabri, Fish
Market, Vishwas Park and MatialaVillage. Further, there are 12 smaller UGRs
under Bodella main UGR which supplies water to Vikaspuri area. A map of
distribution lines emanating from the WTP is placed at Annexure-3.2.

Pumping of water from various UGRs is handled by two E&M divisions viz.
West and South West divisions. Apart from water from Nangloi, West
Division also handles water from Haiderpur and Wazirabad WTPs. Similarly,
South West Division also handles water from Haiderpur and Sonia Vihar.
Further, areas under three out of four civil divisions which receive water from
Nangloi,also receive water from Haiderpur WTP. This is due to the fact that
the jurisdictional areas of civil divisions have been allocated on the basis of
Assembly constituencies. One distribution line from Bodella UGR branches

90

into two and both the branches supply water to areas under both West-I and
West III divisions. Similarly, water from CT-2 (DDA UGR) is supplied to
areas under West-1, West-II] and South West I divisions through a single
distribution line which snakes through all these divisions. In this set up, water
received by each civil division can not be measured

As per information furnished by the four civil divisions receiving water from
Nangloi WTP, the total population in the command area of WIP Nangloi was

Revenue earnings

3.1.5.1 Metering of water supply

In order to keep a watch on consumption patterns and realisation of revenue,
proper measurement of supply of water for domestic, commercial and
industrial purposes is essential.

As per information furnished by DJB (August 2012), there were 19.64 lakh
connections out of which 6.79 lakh were un-metered/without functional
meters which constituted about 35 percent of the total connections. Apart
from ensuring proper realisation of revenue, metering will also increase
efficiency in use of water, detection of leakages in the system and enable high
end consumers to be charged for extra consumption. Supply of water to such
a large number of consumers without meters/with defective meters is
detrimental to optimum utilisation of the available potable water apart from
depriving DJB of revenue.

In its reply (March 2013), the DJB stated that it had sanctioned a proposal for
procuring and installing eight lakh domestic water meters meeting
international standards and tenders for procurement of four lakh water meters
had already been received. However, the reply is silent as to when the DJB
would be able to achieve metering of remaining water connections

Non-revenue water is water which does not earn any revenue. This could be
due to different reasons like unauthorised consumption through illegal
connections, water thefi, metering inaccuracies, authorised consumption not
billed such as public taps and also real losses through leakages in the
transmission and distribution networks. Reduction in non-revenue water will
not only improve financial sustainability of the DJB but also reduce loss of
water which can be used to meet currently unsatisfied demand or future
demand of potable water.

In terms of Ministry of Urban Development, Gol Handbook on Service Level
Benchmarking, the acceptable extent of non-revenue water is considered as 20
per cent. However, the extent of non-revenue water in DJB was more than
three times the prescribed limit during last three years as shown in Table 3.5.

intent to tackle the problem of excessive non-revenue water.

any data relating to the effect of these factors on non-revenue water. Further,
the extent of water supplied through tankers and tubewells was less than 15
per cent whereas the extent of non-revenue water was more than 60 per cent.
